1293. LEONARD SMITH, EDWARD SMITH, and JOHN THOMAS, were indicted for stealing, on the 15th of April, 300 lbs. of oakum, value 3l., the goods of John Scott.
THOMAS BUNN. I am clerk to John Scott, a ship-chandler, of Narrow-street, Limehouse. I was fetched to the warehouse on the morning of the 15th of April, about seven o'clock, and found Thomas in custody there; I said,"Have you got some oakum?" he said he knew nothing about it, but the two Smiths had asked him to bring two bundles of oakum to our warehouse, and they had each brought two themselves; I know the oakum by one bundle having been cut open on the Saturday before, and on the Monday morning they were all taken away.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Is not Scott a dealer in marine-stores? A. Yes; I only know the oakum by the parcel which is cut open, and it is picked off Russian hemp; I am convinced of it; there is a good deal picked in that way.
Cross-examined by MR. QUIN. Q. Has Scott any partner? A. Not in this trade; the oakum was taken away, and then brought back.
GEORGE WOOD. I am a warehouseman to Mr. Scott. On Saturday night I examined the premises; there was aquantity of oakum there; I can swear to this bundle, as I cut it open and tied it again; I know my own tying. On Monday morning, the 15th, I went there, and found the warehouse-door had been wrenched, and was obliged to take the lock off to get in; the brass handles had been wrenched off; I went up to the loft where the oakum was kept, and found the windows, which had been nailed down, were forced up, and the oakum gone; I came down again, and the Smiths came and asked if I had lost any thing; I said, Yes; they went away, and each brought two bundles of oakum; Thomas then came with two, and said, "That makes the sixth;" I looked at it and knew it; I sent for Brown.
WILLIAM MOBRAY. I am in Mr. Scott's employ. About seven o'clock I saw Thomas; he said to me, "Tell Mr. Brown to pay no one for the oakum but me; and I will stand a tanner or two."
THOMAS WILLIAM COLLENDER. I was sent for, and took Thomas - I took him to Mr. Scott's; he said all he knew was, that the Smiths told him to take the oakum there; that they had stolen it, and he was to sell it, and share the money; he said at the warehouse that he got it from Smiths' father's parlour; and that they rolled it out of the front door.
THOMAS' Defence. These lads employed me to carry it.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18270531-281]
1294. EDWARD ROWDEN was indicted for embezzlement.
SECOND COUNT, for stealing 3 half-crowns and 3 shillings, the monies of Thomas Hollins, his master.
THOMAS HOLLINS. I am a grocer. The prisoner was in my service, and received money for me in my shop, and should put it into the till; Mr. Armstrong marked some money, in my presence, which I gave him.
Cross-examined by MR. BRODRICK. Q. Did any body serve in the shop except the prisoner? A. No; he was frequently left in the shop if he wanted change - I always left a lad in the shop who had got change, if it was wanted, except on this occasion; if he was out he could send the servant for change.
HENRY ARMSTRONG. On the 25th of April, about eleven o'clock in the morning, Hollins gave me three half-crowns and three shillings; I marked them, and sent Hall to purchase 1 lb. of tea and 6lbs. of sugar; I gave him all the money I had marked; the articles came to 10s. - he brought me 6d. change; I afterwards examined the prosecutor's till, when the prisoner was sent out, and found two shillings short of that money - this was in about half an hour; the three half-crowns and one shilling were there; an officer was called in, and when the prisoner came home, I said, "You have robbed your master;" he said, "No, Sir, you are mistaken, I am an honest man; I have only two shillings, which I brought from my wife this morning;" he was searched, and the two marked shillings and two other shillings found on him.
Cross-examined. Q. Was it your money? A. No.
MR. HOLLINS. It was agreed that the purchase was not to stand good; it was only to detect the prisoner; the goods were returned. He said nothing about giving charge.
CURTIS WILLIAM HALL. I was sent by Armstrong to this shop to buy grocery, which came to 10s. - I had no money but what he gave me; I gave the same three half-crowns and three shillings to the prisoner, who served me.
MR. HOLLINS. I left 24s. in the till, and when I returned there was 32s. - I sent him out immediately - he was not gone above ten minutes.
JOHN GROOM. I am an officer, and searched him; he said he had only 2s., which his wife gave him that morning; I found 3s. in his pocket, and 1s. at his foot; I suppose it had dropped.
Prisoner's Defence. My wife gave me 5s. that morning - a person came in who wanted change; I had not enough by 2s., which I took out of my pocket, and afterwards paid myself out of the till.
GUILTY. Aged 29.
The prisoner received an excellent character, and was strongly recommended to mercy.
Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18270531-282]

Fourth Middlesex Jury - Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
1299. THOMAS THOMAS was indicted for on the 7th of May, feloniously, willfully, and maliciously assaulting Rosetta Cohen, spinster, at St. Dunstan, Stepney, in a certain public street and highway there, with intent the garments and clothes, which she then wore, wilfully. &c. to deface against the statute the said garments, being the goods of Hyam Benjamin Cohen .
ROSETTA COHEN. I was twelve years old last January. On the 7th of May I was in York-street, Stepney, and stopped to look at Punch and Judy. I saw the prisoner following me, whichever way I went. I had seen him on the Friday night before, in Whitechapel, but had no quarrel with him - I said nothing to him; I did not see him do any thing - but Smith came and took me into a hairdresser's shop, and told me to feel my pocket and see if I had lost any thing. I found my pelisse was cut as if with a knife - I lost nothing; I had a silver thimble and pencil there.
HYAM BENJAMIN COHEN. This child is my daughter, and resides with me - a gentleman called and took me to York-street; I found her in a hair-dresser's shop with her pelisse cut.
HENRY WHITFIELD. On the 7th of May I was selling shrimps, and saw Rosetta Cohen in the street; I saw the prisoner go up to her, take a knife out of his pocket, and cut her pelisse one way - he then shut his knife, put it into his pocket, and appeared to be stooping to find her pocket-hole; he then took his knife out again and cut it the other way.
GEORGE WATSON. I stood next to the prisoner, and saw him take the knife and cut the girl's pelisse across twice.
RICHARD SMITH. I am a patrol. In consequence of what the witnesses told me I came up, and ask the prisoner into custody. I found no knife d
HENRY WHITFIELD. I am sure he used of knife - it was a sort of square handled knife.
The prisoner put in a written defence, stating that he was merely standing to look at the show; and was perfectly innocent.
GUILTY. Aged 18.
Judgment Respited .
[trialAccount t18270531-287]
1300. JAMES PINN was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of April, 2 coal-hods, value 2l. 2s.; 2 tea-kettles, value 30s., and 1 tea-pot, value 9s., the goods of Richard Levett.
MR. BARRY conducted the prosecution.
RICHARD LEVETT. I am an ironmonger, and live in Broad-street, Ratcliff. On the 16th of April the prisoner called at my shop, and said he was directed by a lady in the neighbourhood to get a coal-scuttle, kettle, and teapot, and would I let her have two of each to show her, and she would return one of each in half an hour - he said"I expect you will give me a few shillings for my trouble." I knew him before; he took the goods, and never returned. I did not see him again till yesterday - I wrote down the prices by his desire - my man delivered them to him.
Prisoner's Defence. I buy goods on commission - I sold them to a person who owes me 16l. - I cannot get the money for them.
RICHARD LEVETT. I never dealt with him before.
GUILTY. Aged 33.
Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18270531-288]
1301. WILLIAM ADAMS and JOHN BARRATT were indicted for stealing, on the 10th of April, 1 watch, value 3l.; 1 shilling, and 1 sixpence, the property of William Perkins.
WILLIAM PERKINS. On the 10th of April I was mending a fence by the road side, at South Mimms - Adams came up, and asked if I had seen any beast go by that day - I said No - he said, "Well, I suppose they are not gone by yet - it is about five o'clock, is it not?" - I took out my watch, and said, "It is." Barratt then passed by, and dropped a needle-case out of his hat - Adams took it up, and asked if it was mine - I said, "No, it is that man's" - he said,"I wonder what he has got in it" - he shook it, then opened it, and there was a needle in it - he took it out, and said,"Now I'll have a little fun, and get a little beer of him" - he then called to Barratt, and said "Have you lost any thing?" he said "Not that I know of" - he then said "Yes, a needle-case;" he called him back, and gave it to him. Barratt took it, touched his hat, and said, "I am much obliged to you," and was going away - Adams said "What is in it?" - he said"A needle;" Adams said he would bet a pint of beer that there was not. Barrett said he would lay 5s. there was; they went on till they got up to a sovereign. Adams asked me to lay the sovereign - I refused, but through his persuasion I did, at last. I gave Adams my watch to hold against the sovereign - the case was opened, there was a needle and pin in it. Adams then gave him my watch - I said"It is a complete swindle, but if you will go home with me I'll give you the sovereign" - they went 40 or 50 yards. Barrett then attempted to run off - I seized him, and they both struck me violently. Barratt exclaimed with an oath,"Knock his head off." Adams struck me violently, and rather stanned me, but as soon as I could, I cried Murder! and still kept hold of Barratt; they then threatened to kill me if I did not let go. Huntley and other people then came up; and then they gave me my watch and 1s. 6d. which I had laid with the watch. I had them secured.
THOMAS HUNTLEY. I was in the road, and heard a great noise. I went up and saw Perkins and Barratt struggling in the road. Adams kicked him several times. I ran up and said "Are you going to murder him?" they made no reply; he said they had swindled him out of his watch.
FREDERICK PROPSTRING. I am a constable, and apprehended Barratt. I found some cups and balls, and needle cases on him.
ADAMS Defence. I gave the watch to the man as soon as he had won it - he caught hold of him and said he would have it back. Barratt called Murder! and I helped him.
BARRATT's Defence. On my way to Barnet I saw the prosecutor in conversation with this man, who is a stranger. I accidentally dropped a case, containing two needles, but did not know it; the prosecutor betted his watch, and 1s. 6d. that it did not contain two needles - it was found to contain one pin and one needle, which he must have placed there - this caused the dispute; he refused to comply, struck me violently, and injured me; after recovering, I returned his watch and money. He demanded 5s. for the insult which I gave him, and went away, but he had me apprehended.
WILLIAM PERKINS. I did take 5s. but was so stunned with the kicks and blows, I did not know what I was about, or I would not have done it.
ADAMS - GUILTY. Aged 38.
BARRATT - GUILTY. Aged 28.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18270531-289]
Before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
1302. RICHARD TAYLOR was indicted for feloniously assaulting William Helder, on the King's highway, on the 2d of April, at St. Giles-in-the-Fields, putting him in fear, and taking from his person, and against his will, 1 watch, value 20l.; 1 chain, value 5l.; 1 ring, value 15s., and 3 seals, value 10l., his property.
MR. ALLEY conducted the prosecution.
WILLIAM HELDER. I am an attorney of Clifford's-inn. On Monday, the 2d of April, between five and six o'clock in the evening, I was going up George-street, St. Giles, and on arriving at the corner of a street which leads to Meux's brewhouse, I received a quantity of mud in my face, eyes, and mouth, from some person, but I do not know who; it got so much in my eyes that I could not see; I was then struck in my stomach, which I drew in, and my watch was immediately taken from my fob; the blow was given by a fist I am certain; it was so sudden; and the watch was taken suddenly. The blow did not hurt me much; I felt my watch go - it was a gold one, and had a gold chain and three seals - it might be worth 40l. or 50l. together; my watch-chain hung out. I saw the person who took the watch, and followed him; it was after the mud was thrown in my eyes that he took it, and whether he came in front of me I cannot say, but I saw him, followed him, and seized him by the shoulder, and am certain that was the person who took it - he had ran about fifteen yards before I took him; he then turned, struck me a violent blow in my mouth, and disengaged himself by struggling; and left a piece of his coat in my hand; I am certain he left it in my hand - I dropped it, pursued, and caught him again, about fifteen or twenty yards further - I then struck him - we had a struggle; he struck me, and he fell from a blow of mine; I fell upon him(this was in one of the narrow streets in St. Giles', I do not know the name) - he then disengaged himself again, and ran off.
Q. How did he get away at that time? A. By struggling on the ground, and crawling on his chest; I followed him again a very little way, and then caught him by the skirt of his coat, and that came off in my hand; I dropped it, and pursued again; he ran about ten yards, and entered a house - I was about to go in, but was surrounded by an immense number of men and women, who had collected; they said if I did I should be most probably murdered, and I declined it; I am not able to say whether the prisoner is the man - I cannot form an opinion about it. I do not know what became of my watch.
WILLIAM SUTTON. I am a cooper, in the employ of Mr. Ladd, a wine-merchant, and live opposite to where this robbery was committed; it was in George-street, a most notorious place; I was in the street, going to the permit-office; I saw the prosecutor coming up the street; there was a young woman following him, soliciting him to give her something to drink - that made me notice him; he put his hand into his right-hand pocket, and gave her a shilling - I was three or four yards off; I noticed, at the same time, that the chain and seals hung a long way down Mr. Helder's thigh; Mr. Helder then passed on, and the prisoner was waiting at the corner of a little street, called Ivy-street; immediately on Mr. Helder's coming up, the prisoner drew his watch from his fob; I saw his hand go up; Mr. Helder said, "You d-nation villian!" and immediately pursued him; he ran round the corner; I am quite sure the prisoner is the man - his face was familiar to me; he and his friends live in the same street as me; I lost sight of him for a moment, while they turned the corner; I ran up, immediately, and when I got to the corner Mr. Helder had hold of him, against the wall, endeavouring to take him into custody; the prisoner struggled with him for half a minute I suppose - they both fell on the ground, and Mr. Helder came nearly between my legs; the contention continued altogether about ten minutes; the prisoner's coat was torn almost to pieces in the contention; they came on to the end of Church-lane, and there Mr. Helder knocked him down; he got up again, and ran up the lane; Mr. Helder pursued him, and I saw no more of them at that time; I returned to my own place, because I had left my door open. I attended at Marlborough-street, nearly five weeks afterwards, and saw the prisoner; I was quite certain of his person. I had seen him walk up the street one evening, about three days after the robbery - he merely walked past.
COURT. Q. You said he lived in the street? A. Yes, with his father and mother; I do not know that they are his father and mother - I did not assist the prosecutor, because I had just recovered from a violent fit of illness - I saw no blow struck previous to taking the watch - I was then five or six yards from the prisoner, and behind Mr. Helder; the prisoner's face was towards me; a blow or push might have been given without my seeing it.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Could a blow have been struck without your seeing it? A. Not if a man had lifted his arm up high; if he had run against him with his hand in his pocket I might not have seen that; if there was a blow it must have been so (making a sort of thrust) - Mr. Helder must be the best judge whether he was struck; I did not see it. The prisoner lives with his friends - I did not give information at any office; but I did to Furzman, the officer, the day following; I both named and described the prisoner, and saw him at the office five weeks after - I may have seen him twice in that interval - Mr. Helder appeared perfectly sober; I was pretty near him, but saw no blow. The prisoner was not an instant in his presence before he took the watch; he was waiting at the corner of the street - all the fighting and struggling I saw was afterwards - I solemnly swear the prisoner is the man, although his life depends on it.
JURY. Q. Did you see any mud thrown? A. I did not, nor do I think there was any thrown - I did not observe any mud, my attention was so much taken with to the contension.
MR. ADOLPHUS to MR. HELDER. Q. This was at six o'clock in the evening? A. A little before six. I was going to my sister's in Bedford-square, and going the nearest way; I had dined at two o'clock; the mud, the blow, and the taking of the watch came all in a moment; it was not a raised blow; he struck me in the belly. I think the blow must have been given with one hand, and the watch taken with the other; the same person did both certainly.
MR. ALLEY. Q. Are you quite certain the blow was given before the watch was taken? A. Yes.
JURY. Q. What sort of a day was it? A. A lowery day; it had rained in the morning and the evening before - it was dirty there.
SAMUEL FURZMAN. I am an officer. Sutton gave me information; he described the prisoner, and mentioned his name; I knew the prisoner before perfectly well; I searched for him both in and out of town, and apprehended him on the 2d of May, and took him to the office.
GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 26.
[trialAccount t18270531-290]
1303. CLIFFORD ELISHA was indicted for embezzlement.
MESSRS. BRODERICK and PRENDERGAST conducted the prosecution.
JOHN AUSTIN. I am one of the trustees of the poor of St. Leonard, Shoreditch , and have been so about twenty-seven years - there are sixty or seventy trustees - the prisoner was the parish-clerk, and he received the burial dues, and money for tombs and vaults; he had a salary of 12l. a-year; he was appointed by the Board of Trustees; I have been a trustee from that time to this; I have received money from him for tombs and vaults, when I was church warden; he accounted very justly to me; his duty was to account for what he received once a month.
Cross-examined by MR. ANDREWS. Q. Was he parish-clerk before the passing of the Act? A. No, after. Mr. Plimley, the vicar, appoints the parish-clerk. Nine trustees form a board - they have appointed a clerk for themselves, that is John Scarr; the prisoner was parish-clerk, but had 12l. a-year for receiving these fees. The church-wardens rendered their accounts when they were called upon, and their accounts are audited by the trustees at their audit committee; there is but one general audit in the year, that is about June; the account is sworn to, and signed by the trustees present; that account should include fees received by the clerk for the burial-ground. There was a struggle between the vicar and the parish about who was to appoint the parish-clerk; it ended in a law-suit giving the vicar the right; the prisoner has had the place ever since. I have not heard of a desire lately to appoint somebody else.
Q. Were you the bearer of a letter from the prisoner to the Board of Trustees after he was accused of dishonesty? A. Yes. I gave it to the chairman.
MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. The contest was about the parish-clerk? A. Yes - a man named Limming then received the parish fees, and then the prisoner did it. The Vicar always expected the clerk to receive the parish fees. I believe the prisoner attended with his books and vouchers in June, when the churchwardens' accounts were audited. I do not know whether he accounted on oath - the churchwarden did. The prisoner accounted to me once a month, when I was churchwarden - he rendered no other account.
COURT. Q. Did he account every month for the receipt of burial fees? A. Yes, and for vaults and tombs - that was his duty, and he did so with me. I was churchwarden in 1819 and 1820. I was a trustee in August 1825.
SAMUEL BLACKBURN. I am a trustee of Shoreditch parish, under the Act of Parliament, and was so the beginning of this year. I was appointed one of the committee to investigate the churchwardens' accounts for the last four years. We had a number of meetings - the business was exceedingly complicated, and took a great deal of time; we began the last week in December, or first in January. Mr. Lane's accounts were investigated - he had been churchwarden, and left off a few weeks after Easter, 1826 - he was churchwarden in 1825. A very long report was made from the committee to the Board, and it was resolved that the prisoner should be prosecuted. I knew nothing of any charge against him before; the investigation was gone into without any idea of a felonious charge. After various applications made to the prisoner, at length these books (looking at them) were produced, under an order from the churchwardens, other applications having failed; here is one containing an account from 1823, to June 1826, it professes to be an account of monies received on account of the churchwardens for graves, vaults, and burials - it contains an account of money received in August, 1825. I have examined it throughout - no credit is given for money received from Mr. Brown, for a grave - there is no account of money received for a grave for Miss Storey - if he received any sum for it, it ought to have appeared here.
Cross-examined. Q. What are you? A. I am at the head of a large establishment.
Q. Are you not a teacher of a congregation? A. I am, of the independent denomination. I was elected trustee seven or eight years ago - I attended pretty regularly. I generally saw Mr. Westall there - I cannot say whether he is more active than myself. I never heard him called the King of the parish. I have no wish about who shall.be parish clerk. I never said I would do all I could to get the prisoner moved out - it is very probable I may have said in conversation with the inquiries of the committee, that such a man ought to be turned out. I am ready to admit that; I have no recollection of saying I would do all I could to get him turned out - I never said so.
Q. Did you not say "I should he glad to hear he had run away and fixed his bail?" A. I never did. I said it was more than probable that he would, having done so on a former occasion. I believe the Grand Jury threw out two bills.
Q. On your solemn oath, did you not say, "It matters not; I will have another bill, or I have not done with him yet?" A. I met Mr. Westall, the church warden, on his return from the Grand Jury. I used no such words to him, nor to Mr. Teal - I have frequently talked with Mr. Teal on the subject. I believe I was one of the first who went before the Magistrate - several of us went; I believe Mr. Searr attended as solicitor. Mr. Harmer was applied to - but, I believe, I and the other trustees thought he had neglected us - we attended on the Magistrate three or four times - there was an application to discharge the warrant when the bill was thrown out, we opposed that - Mr. Harmer attended us; we understood that certain links of evidence had not been gone into; and we merely stated, that the case on which the warrant was granted, had not been brough! before the Grand Jury. I think only one charge was investigated by the Magistrate - several were made. The board ordered hand-bills to be printed to cause the prisoner to be apprehended - I believe I gave the order to the printer, he was taken three or four weeks after. I did not send three or four paupers to take him - he was taken by some paupers.
Q. Can any human being, by looking at this book, tell whether the money paid by Brown, for Miss Storey's grave was paid in or not? A. We looked through all the bills which the church warden rendered - no such entry appears either in the book or the bills, this book was obtained by a particular application from the Vicar. The prisoner afterwards applied for the book, to go over it, and give explanations. I believe his application was denied - I do not recollect having any conversation with Mr. Lane; something was said about a vindictive feeling, and I said if a fine of 1s. would answer the ends of justice, I should be satisfied.
MR. BRODRICK. Q. Were the monthly accounts produced by the prisoner? A. No; by the churchwarden; I saw them - they were in the prisoner's hand-writing; there is no account in any of them for Miss Storey's grave - here are the accounts delivered at the time Mr. Lane was churchwarden, there is no such entry. I believe they are in the prisoner's hand-writing - the names are in ink, but in many instances the figures are in pencil, I only went before the Grand Jury on this bill; when Mr. Westall said the bill was thrown out, I said "The board will not allow the business to rest here - there are too many charges; other bills will be preferred.
COURT. Q. Is this the same charge as the other hills related to? A. No; we could not swear to particular pieces of money in the other cases - what I said about a fine of 1s., was merely to shew that no vindictive feeling actuated us.
PHILIP BROWN. I am an undertaker. I paid a sum to the prisoner's wife for a grave for Miss Storey, on the 25th of August, 1825; I paid a sum of 5l. 5s. - I gave her five sovereigns - I got a receipt a fortnight or three weeks after - I do not know whose hand-writing it is.
Cross-examined. Q. State, as well as you can, what portion you paid in sovereigns? A. It was in five sovereigns; I paid it to Mrs. Elisha at his house.
SAMUEL BLACKBURN. This receipt is in the prisoner's hand-writing. (Receipt read.)
THOMAS LANE. In 1825 I was a churchwarden. I allowed the prisoner 12l. that year for receiving the dues - he rendered me an account of them; but not regularly, every month - there was sometimes a delay; when they were delivered, they were called over with this book - there is no account of a grave for Miss Storey - it should he there, I presume; but previous to my being in office they were not so regularly kept. I received the sums, entered here, at various times, but not always when the book was given in - he balanced his account at the close of the year; there is no sum of five guineas entered.
Cross-examined. Q. Though there is no specific entry, can you take upon yourself to swear that neither him nor his wife ever accounted to you for it? A. I cannot tell what he might have paid it from (looking at a receipt for 35l.) - I do not know whose writing this is - here is written, on the back, ten guineas and five guineas. I would not swear this five guineas has never been paid.
The Court ruled that there was no proof of the money coming to the hands of the prisoner, in the particular specie charged.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18270531-291]
1304. THOMAS JONES was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of March, three 50l. and two 20l. Bank notes, the property of William Jones from his person.
WILLIAM JONES. I am a weigher at the Custom-house - I have known the prisoner some years; he belonged to the Two penny Post-office, in Gerrard-street. On the 28th of March I received 355l. 16s. 2d. at the Bank, by an order from the Court of Chancery, a suit having been decided in my favour - the prisoner went with me to receive it - I got five 50l., five 20l., five 5l. notes, and 16s. 2d.; it was between eleven and one o'clock, and the prisoner received 77l. on the same suit - his brother and brother's wife, his sister's husband, Mr. Gregory, and his clerk, were with us; I put the notes into my trousers pocket, all in a lump together. I went to Mr. Hill, my lawyer, in Welbeck-street, and paid him two 20l. notes, and he gave me 5l. in change. I walked a little way; we then all got into a coach, I think the prisoner was on the box - he waited at a public-house while I went in to Mr. Hill's - I then went there to him, and was intoxicated - I did not come to my senses till next morning, when I was at my own house, in Draper's-buildings, London-wall - I missed three 50l., and one 20l. note; the others were still in my pocket, but rather open, not wrapped up as I had put them in - I went to the prisoner's house at Paddington, in two or three days, with an officer twice, but could not find him.
Cross-examined by MR. BRODRICK. Q. There was alarge party of you? A. Yes; after receiving the money we went and dined at a public-house, opposite the Mansion-house - I took out a 20l. note there, and paid Bell 18l., and I lent Mr. Heck a 20l. note; I was intoxicated; I remember coming out and saying, "Oh! come, I am trumps - I have lent him the money" - I lent no other money - I have found out Heck's lodging, and they tell me he has not been at home for some weeks.
ROBERT BELL. I am clerk to Mr. Gregory, the solicitor - I went with them to the Bank, and saw the money paid, and from the state the prosecutor was in, I was afraid he might be robbed, and desired the cashier to give him five 50l. notes, as they could be better traced - he put his money into his trouser's pockets - we went into the Mansion-house public-house - Mr. Gregory wished us to stay by him, as he owed him 18l. - we had 3lbs. of beef-steaks and some porter - he put a 20l. note on the table, and I desired the persons there to mind that it was a 20l. - the prisoner was one of the company - I took the note (Heck then called him out) - I think he put his notes back into his pocket - we took a coach in Cheapside, and I went with him to Welbeck-street (the prisoner was on the box) - I saw him take out the notes, which were wrapped up closely - he there paid two 20l. notes - I then went with him to another public-house, staid a few minutes, and then left them, exactly at five o'clock.
Cross-examined. Q. When Heck called him out, did you go with him? A. No; when he came in he said he was trumps, and had lent the money - I have heard that Heck is absent - the prisoner is a letter-sorter.
- GREGORY. I am an ironmonger. I was with Bell - his account is correct - I left them at the Mansion-house public-house.
WILLIAM MOORE. I am a shoemaker, and live in Oxford-street. On the 29th of March, between eight and nine o'clock in the morning, the prisoner came to the shop with two men and two women, who said they wanted some boots and shoes, and could I give them change for a 50l. note, or they must go into the City - I fitted them all but one man; a young man who came with the prisoner paid me a 50l. note - I asked for the name and address - the prisoner himself wrote in it, "Jones, Bank of England, Prade-street, Paddington," and I wrote "March 29, W. M." I gave the change to the young man.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you see a foreigner there? A. Yes; he came in after the note was produced, I think - when I asked for the address, one of the women said to the prisoner, "Put your name and address."
HENRY SELWOOD. I am clerk to Mr. Hill. The prisoner paid me two 20l. notes - I gave him 5l.; he had other notes in his hand; they were new notes.
JAMES LANGRIDGE. I am shopman to Mr. Blackman, a linen-draper, of Judd-street; the prisoner came to my shop with two women, the latter end of March, or beginning of April; they bought 2l. or 3l. worth of goods, and he gave me a 20l. note, which I gave Mr. Blackman, who gave him change - I did not ask the prisoner for his address, but he gave it to master, as "Bishop Cromer-street" - I did not see master write the note.
- BLACKMAN. I remember some persons coming to my shop - I cannot say the prisoner was one - I received the note, and paid it away to Messrs. Leaf and Severs, Old Change; he mentioned the name of Bishop - I put my initials on it, when I paid it away.
Cross-examined. Q. Can you swear to that note? A. Yes - because I had no other at that time; I paid it away eight or nine days after - I had no other 20l.; I only know I paid them the same note; I sent it by my man; I have not been to see if Bishop lives in Cromer-street.
GEORGE DYER. I am a clerk in the Bank - I produce a 50l. note, and a 20l. note; the 50l. has "Jones, Praed-street, Paddington," on it.
MR. BLACKMAN. This is the 20l. note, and has my initials - I had no other.
MARY MOSS. I am house-keeper to the prosecutor. On the 28th of March, about half-past nine o'clock, he was brought home in a coach, by five people, very drunk; they left him at the corner of the buildings, and fetched me to get him home; the prisoner was one of them - also Heck and his wife; they staid till a quarter-past ten o'clock, and next morning he complained of being robbed.
JAMES BOQUET. I am a clerk at the Bank. I paid this money to Jones, but the clerk who entered the notes, is not here.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18270531-292]
1305. WILLIAM CONDELL was indicted for a burglary in the dwelling-house of George Dobree, about the hour of ten in the night of the 1st of March, at St. Pancras, with intent to steal, and stealing therein 6 coats, value 10l.; 525 yards of silk, value 59l.; 4 pairs of trousers, value 50s.; 5 handkerchiefs, value 6s.; 21 yards of cambric, value 5l.; 8 shawls, value 59l.; 3 scarfs, value 12l.; 1 veil, value 10l., and 1 pelisse, value 20s., his property.
The prisoner pleaded GUILTY - DEATH.
[trialAccount t18270531-293]
1306. WILLIAM CONDELL, GEORGE PLUMMER, and ELIZABETH (THE WIFE OF THOMAS) HARMAN were indicted for burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Samuel James Wood, about the hour of twelve, in the night of the 2d of April, at St. James, Clerkenwell, with intent to steal, and stealing therein 4 coats, value 4l. 10s.; 2 waistcoats, value 10s.; 6 pairs of trousers, value 5l.; 2 necklaces, value 7s.; 2 bracelets, value 1s.; 2500 yards of lace, value 81l.; 20 caps, value 40s.; 14 veils, value 7l.; 2 umbrellas, value 12s.; 1 handkerchief, value 2s.; 9 lace collars, value 40s., and 36 cap-crowns, value 40s., his property.
CONDELL pleaded GUILTY - DEATH.
MR. ANDREWS and MR. ADOLPHUS conducted the prosecution.
SAMUEL JAMES WOOD. I am a pawnbroker, and live at No. 35, St. John-street, in the parish of St. James, Clerkenwell, and rent the house. I keep my pledges in the top room of my house. On Tuesday, the 3d of April, between seven and eight o'clock in the morning, I discovered that it had been broken open; the thieves had taken a square of glass out of the sash of the top-room window, and opened the sash; two iron bars were then taken off and a padlock, which fastened them, was wrenched off; they could get along the gutter from an empty house next door; one shelf of lace was completely emptied - I missed the articles stated in the indictment, among others; they were worth more than 100l. - I had examinedthe padlock the day previous, or the day before that, it was safe.
Cross-examined by MR. DOWLING. Q. Have you any partner? A. No; it was quite daylight when I discovered it; I had been in the room on the Monday afternoon, and saw every thing safe; the bars were fastened inside - they opened the sash, and broke the bars, I think.
MARY ANN VANDEVELDE. In April last I lived with the prisoner Plummer, in Pettiford-court, Drury-court, Strand - the prisoner Harman lived with Condell, in Grange-court, Clare-market. On Monday night, the 2d of April, I was at Condell's, in Grange-court, with Plummer - Condell and Harman were there; I heard Condell ask Plummer to go with him to Mr. Wood's; I had heard it mentioned two or three nights before; Condell said they could go and get money at Mr. Wood's, and would Plummer go with him, and it was agreed that we should meet on that Monday night. On Monday night Condell asked Plummer to go with him - he said they were to open the door of an empty house next door, and get in from the roof of the house; I went with them - Condell did not say I was to go.
Q. Did not Condell and Plummer go out on Sunday night? A, Yes; they came back, and said they had been to Mr. Wood's, but could not do it, as there were women about. On Monday we went and had tea at Condell's, when he asked Plummer to go; he said he did not mind; I and Harman went with them; we set out about eight o'clock or a little after - we went on to Thompson's, in Holborn, and there had some gin; Harman said she should like to go with them - Condell said he did not mind, and it was agreed we should all go together; we went straight to Mr. Wood's- I think it had struck nine when we got there; it was dark - I did not know the house; Condell pointed out the empty house, but did not go in then; we went and had some beer at a corner public-house, and staid there till about ten o'clock; we then came out, and walked up and down. - Condell said, "It is too soon yet;" we all four walked about till near twelve o'clock; Condell then opened the door of the empty house, with a crow-bar; there was no one about at that time; a coach was going by, which prevented the noise being heard - the door opened a little way, and they put in pieces of wood; Condell and Plummer went into the house - Harman and I staid outside - it was agreed before that we should not go in. The door was fastened after they went in - Harman tried it; we two then went straight home. We left our place again about four o'clock, and got there about five; they had not desired us to come back; we went of our own accord; it was them just daylight. We went up to the empty house; there was a coach going by at the time - Harman coughed, as a signal for them to come out, and they came out with two bundles; they said they contained lace; we passed over to St. John's-square; Plummer gave me one bundle, and Condell gave Harman the other; we took them to Condell's lodging; Harman said, when any one came by, "We had better make haste, or we shall be too late for the coach." We got to Condell's near upon six o'clock; Condell opened the bundles there - there was a great deal of lace, caps, and veils; there were three coats; Condell had one of the coats on when he came out of the house, and a pair of trousers on; there were three pairs of trousers; Plummer also had a pair of trousers on; there was some necklaces and bracelets; Harman gave me some of them - they opened them all, and put them into a box in the room; there was some lace, veils, and caps, tied in a yellow handkerchief; there was also two umbrellas; I went out, and pawned a pair of trousers, and Harman went and pawned a coat at Cameron's; I went into the shop, and Harman into the box; Condell asked me to pawn them - we went straight home to Condell's lodging. The officers came there as soon as we had done our breakfast; they handcuffed us three together, but Plummer was not there; he had gone out about two minutes before they came; Avis broke the box open, and Clements found the bundle. A man named Williams came there, and had some tea and gin.
Cross-examined. Q. How old are you? A. Nineteen last Saturday; I have come from the House of Correction; I have been there nine weeks; I am an unfortunate girl; I first told this story on Easter Saturday - I had then been in custody a week and a few days; I was the first to communicate it; the officers did not ask me to do it; I have no annuity, or any thing but what my mother sends me - she has 2l. a month for me, but keeps it till I am of age - Mr. Oliver pays it. I have come here to save myself, not to save my annuity; I know I should lose it if I was convicted. - Plummer is nineteen years old to-day.
MR. ANDREWS. Q. How long had you known Plummer? A. I had lived with him for twelve months.
COURT. Q. How long after they went into the house did you remain there? A. Five or ten minutes; we then went home, and when we went in the morning we waited about ten minutes before they came out.
MR. WOOD. I think it must have taken them an hour to break into the house.
GEORGE AVIS. I am an officer of Marlborough-street. On Tuesday morning, the 3d of April, about half-past eight o'clock, I and Clements went to Condell's lodging, in Grange-court, into the front parlour, on the right-hand; we found Condell, Harman, and Vandevelde there; I went up to Condell, said I wanted him, and handcuffed him; Clements took the others; I found a number of articles in this box (producing it) - I found a crow-bar, a picklock, a centre-bit, and punches - they are such tools as house-breakers use; it is not a complete set; I have had the property ever since. Condell said the box belonged to him; I asked him for the key - he said he had lost it, and that the box had been brought that morning, by two men, named Welch and Smith (Vandevelde was there); I asked Condell if he knew where they lived - he said No; he said the implements were brought in by the same men; I then got Mrs. Westcoat to search the women; she gave me a duplicate, which she said she took from Vandevelde - also a pair of bracelets, and in amber necklace; Vandevelde said Harman had given them to her - Condell was present. I also found in a cupboard in the room, some duplicates of lace, and different things.
Cross-examined. Q. They were all in Condell's lodging? A. Yes. A pair of blue trousers were found on Plummer.
THOMAS CLEMENTS. I am an officer, and went with Avis. Condell and the two women were there; I found these two umbrellas, and a bundle in a yellow handkerchief, containing ten black veils, seven white veils, fourteen collars, seven yards of lace, and fifteen pieces of lace; Mrs. Westcoat found in Harman's pocket a cap and twelve cap-crowns; I found some cruets in a cupboard.
MARY WESTOOAT. My husband is an officer. I searched Harman, and found in her bosom a lace cap, which I gave to Clements - she said she put it there to get some gin. I found the bracelets and necklace in Vandevelde's bosom, and a duplicate in her shoe.
JOSIAH SHERGOLD. I was with the offiers. I saw the property found. I produce three coats, two pairs of trousers, and thirty-seven duplicates, which I found at Condell's- I saw Avis find the tools. On Saturday I went to Gibralta-row, Prospect-place, Tothill-fields, to see Sarah Horsford, and while there Plummer came in; he had been in the house, and been down the yard, and as he came back Horsford said, "Oh, my God! there is George;" Plummer was then within hearing; I took him, and found on him a gold pin, a silver pencil-case, and a knife - Mr. Wood claims part of them; I asked Plummer his name - he said Wilmott, and asked what I wanted him for; I told him he was accused of robbing Mr. Balfour, Mr. Wood, and Mr. Dobree - he said he knew nothing of it.
SAMUEL SALT. I am a watchman, and went with Avis to Condell's, and assisted in searching the room - I found fifty-one duplicates belonging to Mr. Wood - I afterwards went with Shergold to Plummer's, in Pettiford-court, and there found some picklock-keys, a phosphorus-box, a turn-screw, a centre-bit (such as housebreakers use), and forty-four duplicates.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you know it was Plummer's lodging, except from what the landlady said? A. No; it is No. 2.
M. A. VANDEVELDE. I lived at No. 2, in that court with him.
MARY ANN BUCKLER. I live in Rosamond-street, Clerkenwell. I pawned this veil at Mr. Wood's on the 19th of March, for 5s. - I went for it again, and could not have it - I am certain this is it - I have the duplicate of it.
Cross-examined. Q. What do you know it by? A. The pattern - I did the hem of it myself, and know my work.
Mr. WOOD. I know this umbrella very well, and have no doubt of this lace - I keep a duplicate on the goods. These duplicates, found at Condell's, were in my house on the Monday night.
JOSEPH HENDERSON. I lived in Kennington-lane. I pawned these nine pieces of lace at Wood's, in June last.(These were part of the goods found at Condell's).
JOHN COX. I pawned some lace at Mr. Wood's; some of this I believe to be mine; it is the same pattern, but it being off the cards, I cannot swear to it.
HENRY POWELL. I am a shopman to Mr. Cameron, of the Strand. Vandevelde pawned these trousers with me, and Harman pawned this coat at the same time - I gave this duplicate to Harman.
WILLIAM STEVENS. I am a shopman to Mr. Cameron. I took the trousers from Vandevelde, and gave her this duplicate.
GEORGE AVIS. I found this pair of trousers on Plummer.
MR. WOOD. I believe these trousers to be mine. I had several pairs of them - I cannot swear to them - no one article is worth 40s.
M. A. VANDEVELDE. Plummer told me, when he came home, that he had got a pair of Mr. Wood's trousers on.
PLUMMER's Defence. My Lord, I should wish respectfully to submit a few words, in pollution of the offence I am charged with. I have laboured hard through life, but I have had the misfortune to become acquainted with this wretched character, who is trying to swear away my life - I have kept her by my industry, little knowing her extravagant ways - she has had acquaintances at my lodgings, which I was utterly ignorant of. She has had a brother found guilty at this bar; another brother, a reputed thief, and herself, a common prostitute, I trust you will consider these things, and that she has been prompted to attempt to prove this to work my destruction.
Two witnesses gave Plummer a very good character.
PLUMMER - GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 19.
HARMAN - NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18270531-294]
1307. WM. CONDELL was again indicted for a burglary in the dwelling-house of William Alexander Balfour, about the hour of ten in the night of the 21st of July, at St. Paul, Covent Garden, with intent to steal, and stealing therein 11 pairs of trousers, value 4l.; 3 waistcoats, value 10s.; 33 yards of cloth, value 16l.; 40 yards of kerseymere, value 5l.; 1 pair of breeches, value 10s.; 3 handkerchiefs, value 3s.; 1 shawl, value 5s.; 1 pair of spectacles, value 6s.; 18 yards of silk, value 2l.; 4 yards of satin, value 6s., and 1/2 a yard of cambric, value 3s., his property; and ELIZABETH HARMAN was indicted for feloniously receiving, on the same day, and at the same parish, the same goods, knowing them to have been feloniously and burglariously stolen.*
*Plummer was included in this indictment, but not tried.
CONDELL pleaded GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 38.
MESSRS. ANDREWS and ADOLPHUS conducted the prosecution.
WILLIAM ALEXANDER BALFOUR. I am a pawnbroker, and live in Chandos-street, in the parish of St. Paul, Covent-garden. On the night of the 21st of January, my house was broken open. I went all over the warehouse the evening before, at seven o'clock, and at eight the next morning, I was told by one of my boys that the house was broken open; the persons had broken through a trap door which led to a loft, and from thence to my warehouse; they could not break the warehouse door, but broke through the ceiling; there was an empty house adjoining, and a pair of trousers, and one or two duplicates were found in that house. I lost a number of articles, worth 60l. or 70l.
MARY ANN VANDEVELD. About three nights before Mr. Balfour was robbed I heard a talk about it. I and Harman staid at Condell's lodgings while Condell and Plummer went to Mr. Balfour's - it was one Sunday evening, about eight o'clock at night - they returned about six in the morning, and said they got into the empty house, and got in at the roof of the house; they had taken a dark-lantern with them, also two crow-bars and some pieces of wood; they brought back some cloth, and some silk, a shawl, some breeches, waistcoats and cambric. Condell gave me some cambric, which I pawned at Mr. Cameron's, for 3s. (two or three weeks after - I have lost the duplicate - Harman was there at the time.
HENRY POWELL. I live with Mr. Cameron a pawnbroker. I produce some cambric pawned by Vandeveld, on the 7th of February - I knew her before.
SAMUEL TAUNTON. I am inspector of the patrol. I went to Mr. Balfour's house on the 22d of January, andfound it had been entered by some persons who came over the parepet of the adjoining empty house, and in that house I found a pair of trousers, a jacket and hat - Mr. Balfour did not claim them.
MARY ANN VANDEVELD. I do no not know these clothes.
ANN BRYANT. My husband is a tailor. I know this shawl to be mine; I pawned it at Mr. Balfour's.
ELIZABETH LUKIN. This handkerchief is mine, it was wrapped round some cotton, which I pawned at Mr. Balfour's.
MARY ANN VANDEVELD. I know this shawl and handkerchief were part of the things they brought away.
GEORGE AVIS. I found this handkerchief at Harman's lodging - she said it was her's.
HARMAN - GUILTY. Aged 39.
Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18270531-295]
Third Middlesex Jury - before Mr. Common Sergeant.
1308. HENRY GODFREY, ABRAHAM BANKS, and JAMES SMITH, were indicted for stealing, on the 19th of May. 20 bushels of oats, value 6l. 15s.; 4 bushels of beans, value 25s., and 1 sacks, value 12s., the goods of Joshua Horlock.
JOSHUA HORLOCK. I reside at Finchley. On the 19th of May Matthews gave me information - I went to my loft about four o'clock, and missed one sack of beans, and five of oats.
WILLIAM MATTHEWS. I am in Mr. Horlock's employ. and took care of his horses. On Friday, the 18th of May, about seven o'clock in the evening, I saw five sacks of oats, and one of beans in the loft - I locked the loft and took away the key, and next morning, at a quarter-past five, I went there, and they were all gone; I knew the prisoners by sight - I believe they are labouring men - the lock was not forced - they had got over the chaise-house door, and could then enter the loft - I had not seen them about the premises.
ROBERT SWAN. I am hostler at the Swan at Finchley. On Saturday, the 19th of May, about two o'clock in the morning, I saw Godfrey and another man run down a lane by our house, and fetch up a cart belonging to James Smith; they ran over the common with it, towards London; Mr. Horlock's house is between there and London; at half-past two o'clock I was going to bed, and saw them come back; I opened the window and looked out; I saw Banks, Godfrey, and another, whom I did not know; the cart was then full of something brown; they were then going towards Barnet; one of them told me to put my bl - y head in and shut the window down; Wells called a few days after; I did not see the cart brought back.
JOHN WELLS. I drive the Boston van. On Saturday morning, the 19th of May, about ten minutes past two o'clock, I was driving through Whestone-gate, and met a cart by the eighth mile-stone, with one man at the shafts, and two pushing behind; I did not observe their persons - there was something brown in the cart.
CHARLES WOODWARD. I live at Thomas Attfield's, and drive the Leeds waggon - I went through Barnet at twenty minutes to four o'clock on the morning of the 19th of May - when I got to Prickler's-hill I saw a cart coming behind me, and four men pushing it; they overtook me; the cart was then empty - Godfrey and Smith were pushing it - I said, "It seems heavier than a wheelbarrow;" Godfrey said it did. a bl - y sight; they were coming towards London; the other two men had passed me just before; Banks was one of them; I asked the man who was with him, where he had been; he said he had been unloading a load at the bottom of the hill, and he had another to unload.
JAMES MATTHEWS. I am a constable of Finchley. The prosecutor gave me information, on the 19th of May - I went to his premises, and traced footsteps across the fields into the road - I saw some oats spilt near the gate, and traced a narrow-wheeled cart from near the edge - I went and looked at Joseph Smith's cart, which has narrow wheels, and in the cart was a few oats - I apprehended the prisoners - Smith said, "You take me, and don't say for what;" Banks said, "You dragged the man's cart, without a horse, with his oats in it;" Banks said, as they were going to prison, "If they had wrote it down for Swan he could not have spoke otherwise than he did."
JOSEPH SMITH. I live near Whetstone, and have a narrow-wheeled cart, which I keep down the lane, near the Swan public-house - I heard of this robbery on Saturday - I found my cart in its right place, about half-past seven o'clock, and twenty or thirty oats in it - I at times have oats in it.
MR. HORLOCK. My house is eight miles from London, and about three quarters of a mile on this side the Swan.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18270531-296]
1309. GEORGE FAGE was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of May, 2 coach wheels, value 35s., the goods of Samuel Hobson and others.
JOHN BEACH. I am a wheelwright, in Mr. Samuel Hobson's employ - he has other partners, the premises are in Long-acre. I missed two coach wheels, which I afterwards saw on Fisher's cart; the prisoner was frequently about our premises, he was employed by Mr. Davy, to whom one of the wheels belonged. I know these two wheels - the fellow of one is at the factory now.
JOHN LENSEY. The prisoner sold me these two wheels on the 18th of May; he said he bought them of a gentleman's coachman - I took them home, Fisher bought them of me.
JAMES FISHER. I bought two wheels of Lensey - Beach has claimed them since.(Property produced and sworn to.)
SAMUEL FURZMAN. Mr. Davy brought the prisoner to the watch-house - he said he bought the wheels, and had had them some time.
GUILTY. Aged 35.
Recommended to Mercy. - Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18270531-297]
1310. GEORGE MAYHEW was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of May, 1 cap, value 7s., and 2 pieces of leather, value 10s., the goods of John Burrows, his master.
ROBERT BURROWS. I am the brother of John Burrows, a boot-maker of Bond-street. The prisoner was in his employ; these articles were missed on the 17th of May, from a box in the parlour.(Property produced and sworn to.)
BENJAMIN WM. VALENTINE. I am an officer. I went to the prisoner's room, and found this property; he said theleather was his own, and that his wife had brought the cap home.
Prisoner's Defence. I did a little work on my own account. I only earned 10s. a week with him. I have had the leather three years, and can produce a pair of my children's boots made from the leather.
B. W. VALENTINE. He said he had bought the leather some time ago.
ROBERT BURROWS. Our private mark is on it.
GUILTY. Aged 36.
Recommended to Mercy. - Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18270531-298]
1311. SAMUEL ASHTON was indicted for stealing, on the 11th of April, 2 books, value 3s., the goods of John Chidley.
JOHN CHIDLEY, I am a bookseller, and live in Goswell-street. On the 11th of April, between one and two o'clock, I was in the parlour, I heard a footstep, and saw the prisoner going out of the shop; I went and overtook him, putting these two books into his bosom.(Property produced and sworn to.)
The prisoner pleaded distress.
GUILTY Aged 38.
Recommended to Mercy. - Confined Three Weeks.
[trialAccount t18270531-299]
1312. HANNAH BARBER was indicted for stealing, on the 29th of May, 1 watch, value 3l., and 2 keys, value 1l., the goods of William Thompson, from his person.
WILLIAM THOMPSON. I am a seaman, and have been on shore about a fortnight. On the 29th of May, about eleven o'clock at night, I met the prisoner in Ratcliff-highway. She asked me home - I went into a house with her. I took out my watch and put it into my pocket. I went to bed with her; in about three minutes, a person came to the door, she called out to that person, then jumped out of bed and ran down-stairs as fast as she could - there was no light - I had not pulled off my clothes; the watch was in my pocket. I got up and missed it - went down and called the watchman. I have not found it; there was a light when I went there, but she put it out before we went to bed.
JOHN FOLKER. I am a watchman. I saw Thompson, about eleven o'clock at night; he said he had been robbed, and shewed me the house - I found nobody at home, but a candle was burning; he described the girl, and gave me his address - I found the prisoner and took her to him; she has lived in that house about two months - Thompson was perfectly sober.
GUILTY. Aged 35.
Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18270531-300]
1313. WALTER CREAK was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of June, 5 shillings, and 4 sixpences, the monies of Elizabeth Reid, his mistress.
ELIZABETH REID. I am a baker. The prisoner was in my employ. On Sunday night Green marked some money for me, and put it into the till, by my desire - the prisoner had no business at the till; on Monday morning five marked shillings and four sixpences were found on him by the officer - I had locked the till.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. How long has he lived with you? A. Twelve months; his character was good; he had 10s. a week and his board.
CHARLES GREEN. I marked 15s. by Reid's desire; the next morning I went to the officer, and had the prisoner taken; this silver is what I marked.
WILLIAM CLARKE. I am an officer. I was fetched, and found this money on the prisoner, with a key which opens the till.
The prisoner received a good character.
GUILTY. Aged 18.
Fined 1s. and Discharged.
[trialAccount t18270531-301]
1314. WILLIAM PAFFEY were indicted for stealing, on the 21st of April, 1 jacket, value 18s., and 1 pair of trousers, value 12s, the goods of Thomas Sharp.
EDWARD GINGER. I am servant to Thomas Sharp. I was informed the prisoner had stolen a jacket and trousers - I ran out, and caught him with them; he fell down with them on his arms.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I was crossing Whitechapel; these things laid under the shop-window - I picked them up; a man said Stop thief! I stopped directly.
GUILTY. Aged 26.
Transported for Seven Years.

1315. JAMES BARKER was indicted for stealing, on the 30th of April, 1 cake of tallow greaves, value 4s., the goods of John King.
JOHN KING. I am a tallow-chandler. and live at Islington; a person told me this cake of greaves was stolen from the door; the prisoner was brought back with it.
JOHN GIRDLER. I saw the prisoner and two other bigger lads - I turned my head and saw the other two lads placing the greaves on the prisoner's head; they told him to make haste - I stopped him with it - I heard them say, "You bl - dy fool, take it on your head. and go down Compton-street, and we will meet you there."
Prisoner's Defence. A boy said he would give me a penny, to carry it to Compton-street.
GUILTY. Aged 15.
Whipped and Discharged.

1316. JAMES LOWE was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of June, 1 goose, value 5s., the goods of Joseph Mazoyer.
JOSEPH MAZOYER. I am a poulterer, and live in St. Giles'. On the 4th of June I was in my parlour, and saw the prisoner snatch a goose from the board - I followed. and took him about fifty yards off, with it.
WILLIAM KEAN. I received him in charge; he said Copley, who had got away, took one goose, and told him to take this one.
GUILTY. Aged 13.
Whipped and Discharged.
[trialAccount t18270531-304]
1317. JOHN ASKIN and THOMAS SKINNER were indicted for stealing, on the 1st of June, 1 copper, value 10s., the goods of Robert Hardy, and fixed to his dwelling-house.
ROBERT HARDY. I live in Glasshouse-street, Whitechapel; my copper was fixed safe at ten o'clock at night,on the 31st of May, and next morning it was gone, when I got up; here it is - I am certain of it - I have compared it with the place it came from.
THOMAS GLOVER. I live at the Crown public-house, Glasshouse-street. I got up about five o'clock in the morning, and saw the prisoners walking up and down the street; I saw Skinner get over the gates of Hardy's-yard - Askin walked to and from till he found an opportunity; Skinner then lifted the copper over the pails, and Askin's received it from him - I got two men to assist, and took them both - Hardy claimed it.
THOMAS JUDD. I secured them immediately.
ASKIN's Defence. I was coming from the Docks - a man said, "Lay hold of this, I will pay you for your trouble."
SKINNER's Defence. I was going to work.
ASKIN - GUILTY. Aged 16.
SKINNER - GUILTY. Aged 18.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18270531-305]
1318. JANE BROWN was indicted for stealing, on the 3d of June, 1 bed, value 2s.; 1 blanket, value 1s.; 2 sheets, value 2s.; 1 rug, value 1s. 6d.; 1 saucepan, value 6d.; 1 kettle, value 6d.; 1 poker, value 4d.; 2 pillows, value 9d., and 1 bolster, value 9d., the goods of Francis Tadman, in a lodging-room.
FRANCIS TADMAN. I live in Leonard-street. I let the prisoner a lodging a fortnight ago - I went for my rent, and found the door locked, and the room stripped - I had her apprehended.
WILLIAM EDWARDS. I am an officer. I found the duplicate of a sheet on her - the pawnbroker is not here; the other property has not been found.
NOT GUILTY.

1319. THOMAS BENTLEY was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of June, 1 snuff-box, value 20s., the goods of Radcliffe Pond, from his person.
RADCLIFFE POND. I live at Chelsea. I was in Pallmall on the 4th of June in the evening, and felt a trifling touch at my coat pocket; immediately after the prisoner endeavoured to rush by me; I caught him with my left hand, felt in my pocket, and missed my box, and found him putting it up his sleeve.(Property produced and sworn to.)
EDWARD BOOTHMAN. I took him in charge.
Prisoner's Defence. I saw a boy drop it, and took it up; it was done to lead me into a snare.
GUILTY. Aged 13.
Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18270531-307]
1320. EDWARD EVANS was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of April, 1 pistol, value 4s., the goods of Joseph Smith.
JOSEPH SMITH. I am a gun-maker. On the 13th of April I gave my son a pistol to take to Somers'-town; he returned in about twenty minutes without it, crying.
WILLIAM COLTON. I am a constable. I received information, and went with the little boy, who pointed the prisoner out; I asked him where the pistol was; he denied it; I said I knew he had got it; he then said, "Say nothing about it, I will go, and give it to you;" he took me down Gray's-inn-lane, and gave it me from under several things; I have known him nine years; he behaved well; I believe he was in distress.
Prisoner's Defence. The boy held it up to several people; I thought he ought not to have such a thing, and took it from him.
GUILTY. Aged 16.
Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18270531-308]
1321. FRANCIS WALKER was indicted for stealing on the 4th of June, 14 pieces of brass, value 18d., and 1 piece of copper, value 6d., the goods of Charles Handley, his master.
GEORGE AVIS. I am an officer. On the 4th of June I went to the Britannia public-house, Portland-street, and saw the prisoner come out of the house of Mr. Charles Handley, his master, who was with me; I went and took off his hat, and found this brass in it; Mr. Handley claimed it in his presence; he said he was very sorry he had been guilty of it; his master gave him a good character; but is too ill to be here.
GUILTY. Aged 40.
Confined Three Months.

1322. THOMAS THORP was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of May, 1 whip, value 3s., the goods of Joseph Elcocks.

1323. WILLIAM TURTLE and THOMAS MORLEY were indicted for stealing, on the 10th of April, at St. Pancras, Soper-lane, 3 table-cloths, value 2l. 8s., the goods of Thomas Lewis and John Thomas James, in their dwelling-house.
THOMAS LEWIS. I live in Queen-street, Cheapside, in the parish of St. Pancras, Soper-lane, and am in partnership with John Thomas James - we are linen-drapers. On the 10th of April these three table-cloths were on our front counter in the warehouse, which is part of our dwelling-house - they were missed about eleven o'clock that day, and are worth from 2l. 5s. to 2l. 8s; they were bleached, which make them worth 2l. 8s.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Were they worth 2l. 8s. to sell again? A. No.
THOMAS HAYLOCK. I am a constable of Middlesex. On the 10th of April I was at the end of Ironmonger-lane, and saw the prisoners with another person - I heard the other one say, "It is of no use standing here, we will go to work;" I followed them down Queen-street, and saw Turtle go into the prosecutors' house, No. 7, Queen-street, with nothing in his hand, and come out with these three table-cloths under his arm - I took him directly - the prosecutors claimed them. Morley did not go into the house - they were walking together - I cannot swear that they spoke together.
WILLIAM SMITH. I am on officer. I saw Haylock takeTurtle, and I took Morley; the other went away - I did not see Turtle go in or come out; as there was a disturbance in the street with the carts.
MR. LEWIS. These are our table-cloths, and have our mark on the paper.
Prisoner TURTLE. Morley knows nothing of it.
TURTLE - GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 16.
MORLEY - NOT GUILTY.

WILLIAM THOMPSON. I live in Gloucester-buildings, Walworth. JOHN JACKSON. I am a constable, and received him in charge.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. A lad gave it into my hand.
GUILTY. Aged 12.
