1326. GEORGE HARDING was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of April, 1 trunk, value 10s.; 1 coat, value 30s.; 1 pair of trousers, value 10s.; 12 printed bound-books, value 10s.; 80 sheets of printed paper, value 10s., and 2 pocket-books, value 4s., the goods of George Leonard Swile.
GEORGE LEONARD SWILE. I am a baker, and lodge in a second floor, in Water-lane, Fleet-street. On the 28th of April my trunk, containing this property, was taken from my room - I left it safe at half-past two o'clock - a person was cleaning the room - I returned about twelve at night, and it was gone - I have found none of the things - I had not seen the prisoner there that day - he used to bring coals to my former lodging, in Crown-court, Whitefriars - it was a hair-trunk.
Cross-examined by MR. BARRY. Q. Had you left the servant to clean the room? A. She comes at times to do it.
SARAH SWILE. I am the prosecutor's mother - we keep a little shop at this house. On the 8th of April, about five o'clock in the afternoon, the prisoner came to my room; he looked much confused - he came in at the side door, not at the shop door - he asked the price of a penny pie, then turned round, shoved the door too, and stood with his back to it - he went out at the side door, and seemed alarmed - I do not know whether he went up-stairs; he took a two-penny pie, and went away.
Cross-examined. Q. There was a way to the shop, through the side door? A. Yes, he had no coals with him; I never knew him bring coals to that house.
MARY MERCHANT. I live in Water-lane. I have known the prisoner some time by sight. On the 28th of April, about five o'clock, I was standing at my street door, which is opposite to Swile's - I saw a very respectable man, dressed in blue, walking to and from by the door - the prisoner was inside the passage, and a hair trunk laid in the passage by him, near the door - the man in blue stepped up, took she trunk on his left shoulder, and walked down the lane as fast as he could; the prisoner came out in about two minutes, with two pies in his hand - he was by the trunk when the man took it. I did not say what I had seen till next morning - I had heard of the robbery at twelve o'clock at night; I did not see them speak to each other; it was done momentarily.
PHOEBE PULSFORD. I live with this witness, and keep a school. I know the prisoner, and saw him go into Swile's passage that afternoon - I saw nothing more.
ALEXANDER JOHNSON. I apprehended the prisoner - he denied the charge. I saw nothing more.
MRS. SWILE. I never knew him to bring coals to this house; I have been there six months; he said nothing to me about a trunk being in the passage.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18270531-314]
1327. WILLIAM TAYLOR was indicted for stealing, on the 23d of April, 1 handkerchief, value 1s., the goods of a person unknown, from his person.
JOHN GIRTON. I am a constable of Broad-street. On the 23d of April, about half-past seven o'clock in the evening, I saw the prisoner and another in Cornhill, as the mail coaches were starting - I saw the prisoner go behind a gentleman, and take a yellow handkerchief out of his pocket; I touched the gentleman on his back, and told him what I had seen - he turned to come across the street with me, but my eye was upon the prisoner, and I lost the gentleman; I followed the prisoner and his companion into the Old Jewry - they went down Princes-street, and were coming into Cheapside again; I took hold of the prisoner, and companion ran away; I took the prisoner up Meeting-house-court, and searched him - I found the handkerchief in his hat; he said it was his own - he had no other. I do not know the gentleman's name.
JURY. Q. Was he out of your sight? A. No.
Prisoner's Defence. I had bought the handkerchief on Monday, and gave 2s. 6d. for it - I put it into my hat, and was going down the Old Jewry; this man came and took me - the handkerchief has a blue stain on it; I got into conversation with the other lad, and he went to the pump.
GUILTY. Aged 17.
Transported for Life.
[trialAccount t18270531-315]
1328. JOSEPH GREEN and JAMES SMITH were indicted for breaking and entering the dwelling-house of George Nightingale, on the 30th of April (he and others being therein), and stealing 3 eye-glasses, value 15s., his property.
GEORGE NIGHTINGALE. I live in Leadenhall-street. and rent the house - I am a carver and gilder. On the 30th of April, about three o'clock in the afternoon, I was at home, and saw the prisoner very close at my window, drawing these eye-glasses out; my lad secured them on thespot - they had broken the window to get them out - it was secure before - they are worth 15s.; they threw them down on the pavement.
Prisoner GREEN. Q. Did you see me take them? A. I saw the fingers of somebody.
JOHN DAVIS. I am in Mr. Nightingale's employ. I saw the prisoners at the window, alone; the glass was whole just before. I went out, and secured them both together. I did not see any thing taken - the window appeared to be cut; as soon as I took hold of them Green threw down two eye-glasses off the curb, and Smith threw one down an area; two knives, ground down for the purpose, were found on them at the watch-house.
JOHN FORRESTER. I am a constable, and received them in charge.(Property produced and sworn to.)
GREEN'S Defence. I happened to look into the window - this man came out, and took us.
GREEN - GUILTY. Aged 14.
SMITH - GUILTY. Aged 13.
Of stealing only. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18270531-316]
1329. WILLIAM ADAMS was indicted for a burglary in the dwelling-house of Margaret Coram, in the night of the 9th of May, and stealing 5 snuff-boxes, value 1l. 11s., her property.
MARGARET CORAM. I am a widow, and live in Clothfair; I rent the house - I deal in snuff-boxes and other things. About eight o'clock in the evening of the 9th of May, I was sitting in my shop, near the window, where the snuff-boxes were in a paper case - a pane of glass was cut, which was quite whole before this; I did not hear the cutting, but I saw the case drawn out about two inches; I saw two boys at the window, and could distinguish their features, as there was a great light from the gas; the prisoner was the biggest of the two. I went to the door, and took hold of the prisoner; the other ran away with the boxes; I should think it was light enough to see a person's features. I did not see more than the two boys.
THOMAS DAVIS. I am in the employ of Mr. Smith, a woollen-draper, who lives nearly opposite Mrs. Coram's. - About half-past seven o'clock in the evening of the 9th of May, I was standing in the shop, and saw the prisoner and four more; I watched them till eight o'clock - there is a book shop nearly opposite, which they were looking into- about eight o'clock I saw them opposite Coram's - three of them went one way, and the prisoner and another went up to the shop; they stood there about five minutes - the prisoner had his back to the window, and the other was between him and the window; the other shoved his hand against the window, and then ran away; I could not see whether he took any thing; Mrs. Coram ran out, and asked who had broken the window; I said the prisoner was one of his companions.
JOHN RYLEY. I am an officer. I took charge of the prisoner - he had nothing but a bit of wood.
Prisoner's Defence. I was coming home through Clothfair; a little boy asked me which was Smithfield - I pointed it out to him, and was standing there when the lady came out, and said, "Did you see any body run away?" this man came out, and said I was one that cut the window - a lady came up, and said "Never mind - I will say you did not break it," and she gave me her direction, but I forget it.
GUILTY. Aged 17.
Of stealing only. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18270531-317]
1330. JOHN BOND and JOHN CLARKE were indicted for stealing, on the 28th of April, one handkerchief, value 2s., the goods of John Watson from his person.
JOHN WATSON. I am a linen-draper, and live on Holborn-hill. On the 28th of April, about nine o'clock in the evening I was between Brook-street, Holborn and Gray's-inn-lane; I was walking along, and as I frequently do, I put my hand slightly to my pocket, and found I had no handkerchief - I was not more than two hundred yards from home - I had it when I left home; the moment I missed it I turned round, and saw Clarke going away from me, about five yards from me - I pursued, and collared him. I found my handkerchief in the left breast of his jacket. I dragged him into a shop. I saw a little boy dressed like Bond near him, but a few yards from him - two officers followed me into the shop, and they brought in Bond.
WILLIAM PRITCHARD. I am an officer. I saw the prosecutor in the street, about two hundred yards within the City. I had been watching the two prisoners for an hour. I saw Bond try Mr. Watson's pocket several times, without success, and just as he passed a tailor's shop by Brook-street, Bond took the handkerchief out of his pocket, and ran off directly - I pursued and took him, and found another handkerchief on him, which I had seen Clarke take from a gentleman half an hour before - they were together.
CLARKE's Defence. I picked up the handkerchief.
BOND - GUILTY. Aged 14.
CLARK - GUILTY. Aged 16.
Transported for Life.
[trialAccount t18270531-318]
1331. JOHN KING was indicted for stealing, on the 30th of May, one pair of stockings, value 10d., the goods of John Hamell.
JOHN HAMELL. I am a hosier, and live in Aldersgate-street. On the evening of the 30th of May, at a quarter past nine o'clock, I was serving a lady, and heard a report of a pull, which pulled these stockings down, they hung about two feet within the door, and were pinned with two strong pins. I turned my eye, that way and missed them. I made no noise, but pursued the prisoner, who I caught sight of about ten yards from the house, and stopped him forty yards off with the stockings rolled up in an apron, which was tied before him - there were two others in his company. I had see the prisoner before, and cautioned him to be careful.
WIYLIAM DORRINGTON. I took him in charge with the stockings.(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY. Aged 15.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18270531-319]
1332. WILLIAM BUTLER was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of May, 1 quire of printing crown paper, value 1s. 6d., and 58 sheets of demy, value 3s. 4d., the goods of Thomas White.
THOMAS WHITE. I am a printer, and live in Crane-court, Fleet-street. I kept my paper in the cellar; the house door is generally open all day; the prisoner is a stranger. On the 4th of May, at half-past six o'clock in the afternoon, I heard a cry of thieves, ran down stairs, and found him in custody, in the yard, with this paper.
WILLIAM DON. I am servant to Mr. White. I had occasion to go into the yard, and saw the prisoner in the privy - I asked what brought him there; he said he had occasion to go there; I was not satisfied, came out, and bolted the yard door - I called the other workmen; I saw him, through a broken pane of glass, unbutton his waistcoat, and throw some paper from it, into the dusthole; it was master's; he must have taken it from the cellar.
THOMAS LIGHTFOOT. I am a constable, and took the prisoner in charge.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I went into the yard to the privy, as I was directed there - I never saw the paper.
GUILTY. Aged 22.
Confined Six Weeks.
[trialAccount t18270531-320]
1333. WILLIAM MARSHALL and JOHN OULDS were indicted for embezzlement.
MR. ADOLPHUS conducted the prosecution.
JOSHUA HEARN. I am a coach and waggon proprietor, and keep the King's Arms Inn, Snow-hill; when I receive parcels, I deliver them to the persons to whom they are directed - Marshall was clerk, in what we call the upoffice; he was to keep an account of the goods received by waggon, and to deliver them to the porters for delivery; he had to make out tickets, with the charge on the parcels to be delivered, and to keep an account of what was received for them. Oulds was my carman for the city; it was his duty to receive money for the parcels he delivered; he was his duty to receive money for the clerk named Jessopp; Oulds could not receive that money without the printed ticket delivered to him by Marshall; it was Marshall's duty to enter in a book marked B what parcels he received - I have a weigh-bill, which was made in the country, dated the 30th of March; the carriers make the weigh-bills, and Marshall keeps them.
STEPHEN JESSOPP. I am in the employ of Mr. Hearn, and know Marshall's hand-writing; this ticket (looking at it) is his hand-writing. except the printed part of it - I have no doubt of it. (Read.)
"King's Arms, Snow-hill. 30th March. - 5 sacks, carriage 4s. To be delivered to Brooks and Co. J. Oulds, porter."
On the 30th of March Oulds did not account to me for any money received from Brooks; here is the book marked B., which Marshall kept; it is in his hand-writing; he has not entered 4s. as received by Oulds from Brooks.
ISAAC BUTCHER. I am in the employ of Mr. Brooks, a merchant, of Sambrook-court; this bill was delivered to me on the 30th of March, by Oulds, to the best of my recollection; he often delivered goods; I paid him 4s. according to the ticket; to the best of my belief it was two shillings and a half-crown piece; he said he had no change; I told him he might take the man who had assisted him with the goods, and spend the 6d. over the way; he had delivered five sacks of potatoes and some bacon.
Cross-examined by MR. ANDREWS. Q. Can you, with certainty, say you paid him two shillings and a half-crown? A. To the best of my belief it was - I recollect giving him the 6d. over - I had received parcels from him before, and am certain he brought these - I am sure I gave him one shilling.
GEORGE HAZLEWOOD WORRALL. I am beadle of St. Sepulchre. I took Marshall into custody about a month ago - I neither threatened nor promised him any thing - I told him I took him for making bad entries in Mr. Hearn's book - he told me, at the top of Newgate-street, that he was drawn into this case by the two porters, Oulds and Satchell.
Cross-examined by MR. ANDREWS. Q. What case? A. This case of felony - he said, "I was drawn into it by the two porters" - this was as he was going to Guildhall, to be examined on this charge.
COURT. Q. When he was first taken did he say any thing? A. He fell down on his knees to Mr. Hearn, who said, "I do not want any of that nonsense."
MR. ANDREWS to S. JESSOPP. Q. Do you keep the accounts of the office? A. I receive all monies from the porters - this would be entered in the book B.
Q. How do you receive it? A. Oulds comes to me and accounts for monies he receives for different parcels - Marshall enters it in the book B. I check it off from that book into the cash-book - when Oulds brings the money he pays it to me only - he ought to do it every night; but never less than every other night - he might bring me 3l. or 4l. I have an account of the payment made on the 31st of March, be paid me 4l. 14l. 8d., - I entered that in my cash book, it is not here; it should appear in this book B - the items are entered here and the different sums. I and Oulds cheque them off from the delivery book B. Marshall had nothing to do with the money - he was only to enter the parcels and deliver the tickets to Oulds.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Then, when goods arrive from the country. does Marshall take an account of them and make out the tickets for the porters to deliver? A. Yes; he enters on the ticket the amount charged - and in the evening the porter gives me what he has received, and I tick it off in this book. I enter the gross sum in the cash-book - the fraud could not be effected but by the joint operation of Marshall and Oulds - if Marshall had entered the 4s. in the book, Oulds must have accounted to me for it; as it would have drawn my attention to it, and I should have had to demand the 4s. of Oulds.
MARSHALL's Defence. (written.) I have just to say, that if I made out the tickets it was with proper charges, and if anything happened afterwards the account was made to Jessopp; I knew nothing about it. I have a wife and four children to support.
OULDS' Defence. The way of settling was sometimes twice a week or oftener. As soon as we settle the book we cut the leaves out. I had no opportunity of knowing whether any money was deficient - if any was short I had to make it up.
JURY to ISAAC BUTCHER. Q. At the time you received these bags did you sign a delivery-book? A. No; I asked if he had one, and he said No.
STEPHEN JESSOPP. He had a delivery-book, which hetook it out that day, I cannot say - Marshall made that book out, as he called the parcels over to the porter - the leaves are all here - as soon as they are full they are taken out and filed - they are all here - the goods of this date are entered here, but these are not there.
Four Witnesses gave Marshall a good character, and three for Oulds.
MARSHALL - GUILTY. Aged 35.
OULDS - GUILTY. Aged 54.
Strongly recommended to Mercy. - Judgment Respited .
[trialAccount t18270531-321]
1334. WILLIAM MARSHALL & JOHN SATCHELL were indicted for a like offence.
JOSHUA HEARN. I keep the King's Arms inn. Goods are brought there to be left till called for - we charge 2d. warehouse-room for the first week, and 1d. a-week afterwards; it is Marshall's duty to receive that - Satchell was carman for the west-end delivery - he took goods to the west, and received money for them - Marshall entered the parcels as they came in, and afterwards made out the tickets for the porters to take with them - he entered in the book marked G, the number of parcels which are to be left till called for, and in the book marked E, what are taken out; 10s. received from Mr. Mount on the 6th of April has never been accounted for.
Cross-examined by MR. ANDREWS. Q. It was not his duty to bring it to you? A. No; it was a parcel which the porter carried out - Marshall would not have to receive the money - he was not entrusted to receive money.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Was Marshall to receive money in any form? A. He has received money for goods delivered, but that was contrary to my wish - Satchell was six or seven years with me, and bore a good character.
STEPHEN JESSOPP. I am clerk at this inn. On the 6th of April twenty-seven articles were brought to our place for Mr. Mount, of Foley-place - they came from Swallowfield, in Berkshire - the carriage would be 10s. when delivered - Marshall's duty was to enter them in the book when they went out - I have looked into the book at this date, and several other dates - they are not entered at all; here is an entry in the general ledger, "twelve boxes, Friday, April the 6th, Mr. Mount, K.A. (meaning King's Arms,)" and under it is this line, "To be left till called for, 2s." - I received that 2s. from Marshall.
CHARLES EVETTS. I am butler to Mr. Mount, of No. 3, Foley-place. On the 6th of April I received twenty-seven parcels from Swallowfield, by Satchell - there was another porter in the cart - I paid Satchell four half-crowns, I am certain - there were no other parcels at the King's Arms for which I called and paid - no other parcels came to our house that day - a ticket came with the goods; I put it into my book, and when Mr. Mount settled with me it was put into the fire.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. How are you sure that you paid four half-crowns? A. I remember having the money in my hand - I had sent to the public-house for change. I gave the prisoner 1s. besides, as he asked for some beer. I think I had seen him before, but could not swear it - he might be three quarters of an hour with me - I am sure he is the man.
JAMES POTTER. I am porter to Mr. Hearn. I went with Satchell to Foley-place; he delivered twenty-seven parcels. I did not see what money he received.
STEPHEN JESSOPP. It was Satchell's duty to account to me for what money he received - he never accounted for this 10s.
G. H. WORRALL. I apprehended Marshell. Mr. Hearn charged him with robbing him - he fell on his knees. As we went along he said Satchell and Oulds had brought him into this. I afterwards searched Satchell, and found a book and some blank tickets in it, with pen and ink to fill them up.
SATCHELL's Defence. I paid the money to Marshall.
SATCHELL - GUILTY. Aged 42.
MARSHALL - GUILTY. Aged 35.
Judgement Respited .

1336. CATHERINE ROOK was indicted for stealing, on the 21 of June, 35lbs. of pig iron, value 18d., the goods of William Thompson and others, his partners.
WILLIAM MASON. I am a watchman on Mr. Alderman Thompson's wharf - he has partners. On Saturday evening, about half-past seven o'clock, I was on the wharf, and saw the prisoner in George-alley, about fifty yards off, walking from the premises, with a large bag. I went and stopped her, and found this iron in it - she said a waterman had given it to her; but she had taken it off a pile on the wharf - I know one of the pieces well.
BENJAMIN UNDERWOOD. I am an officer. I took her in charge.
Prisoner's Defence. A man gave it to me.
GUILTY. Aged 55.

[trialAccount t18270531-324]
Fourth Middlesex Jury - before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
1322. JOHN GROUTHER was indicted for stealing, on the 26th of May, 7 sovereigns, the monies of James Lappage, in the dwelling-house of Jane Prince .
JAMES LAPPAGE. I am a mariner. I arrived in England on the 23d of May, from the East Indies - I lodged at Mrs. Jane Prince's, Beavis-street. On Saturday, the 26th of May, I received eight sovereigns, as the balance of my wages - I put them into my pocket-book in my pocket - the prisoner was in the office when I received the money - I had been drinking at a public-house with Simmons, a shipmate - the prisoner was slightly acquainted with him - after receiving my money, we all went down to my lodgings, and Simmons persuaded his sister to dress my foot, which a coach wheel had run over the night before - the prisoner had heard me say my arm was bruised, and he persuaded me to pull my jacket off - he had seen me put my money into my jacket pocket - he helped it off, and soon after I saw him go down the steps into the yard, with a pocket-book in his hand - I thought nothing of it at thetime - I was in such pain - he came in again in a few minutes, put the pocket-book on the chair, put my jacket over it, and ran out of the house - I then took up the book, and there was only one sovereign left; he was taken on the Wednesday - I do not know what parish the house is in.
WILLIAM SIMMONS. I was with Lappage, at my mother's, Mrs. Prince. I saw the prisoner take the pocketbook out of the pocket, but had no suspicion whose it was - he came up-stairs in about two minutes, and laid it on the chair, and away he ran - I once kept company with his sister, before I went to sea - he was then on board a man-of-war.
WILLIAM THOMPSON. I lodge at Prince's. I met the prisoner in Ratcliff-highway on Wednesday, and told him he had robbed my shipmate; he said he had not, and that he did not know me, though he had slept with me on Friday.
WILLIAM SIMMONS. I am an officer, and took him in charge. I found only two sixpences on him.
JOHN SWAIN. I am a watchman, and assisted in taking him.
Prisoner's Defence. I did not see the prosecutor after I got up in the morning.
GUILTY. Aged 27.
Of stealing, but not in the dwelling-house.
Transported for Seven Years.

1338. BRIDGET MAHAGAN was indicted for stealing, on the 8th of April, 1 bonnet, value 10s.; 1 shift, value 2s.; 1 pair of stays, value 1s.; 1 pair of stockings, value 6d.; 2 caps, value 2s.; 1 thimble, value 6d.; 1 pair of scissars, value 6d.; 1 handkerchief, value 1s.; 1 crown, and 2 shillings, the goods of Mary Cane; 1 gown, value 5s., the goods of William Lines; 1 pair of shoes, value 4s.; 9 books, value 10s., and 1 wine-glass, value 6d., the goods of Anthony Logan, in the dwelling-house of Elizabeth Andrews .
MARY CANE. I am servant to a green-grocer, and sleep at Anthony Logan's - the prisoner came there one Monday to lodge - I found her there when I came home - we slept together - she used to go out at four o'clock every morning - she said she was in the milk-trade. On Sunday morning I missed a silk handkerchief and several other things from my box - she had then gone out, and did not return till she was apprehended - she then gave me my silk handkerchief.
ANTHONY LOGAN. I live in this house which is in Angel-court, King-street, Westminster - Elizabeth Andrews keeps the house, and lives two doors off - the prisoner came to lodge there on the 3d of April, and went away on the 8th - I found her that day in Cato-street, with some of the property, and she had my wife's stays on.
THOMAS WILSON. I am an officer, and took her in charge - she told me where the things were pawned, and the pawnbrokers gave them up to me - the bonnet was on her head.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. Cane asked me to pawn the shift.
MARY CANE. I never asked her to pawn any thing.
GUILTY. Aged 21.
Of stealing, but not in a dwelling-house.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18270531-326]
1339. RICHARD JONES LEE was indicted for stealing, on the 8th of May, at St. Mary-le-bone, 3 silver spoons, value 3l., the goods of Henry Young, in his dwelling-house.
WILLIAM HENRY BOND. I am a pawnbroker, and live in Clarence-market, Regent's Park - the prisoner came on the 8th of May, about four o'clock in the afternoon, and offered to pawn three table-spoons - I asked what he wanted - he said 3l. - I asked if they were his own - he said they were, that his name was John Jones, and he lived at Kentish-town - I suspected the property was stolen, and sent one of my men for an officer - I question ed him about a shawl which he had previously pawned - he then opened the door, and ran out - I followed and brought him back again.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Did the prisoner run away entirely? A. No; 1 overtook him sixty or seventy yards off, and secured him - he left the spoons behind him. I swear positively he is the man.
JAMES BROWN. I was servant to Dr. Henry Young, who lives at No. 19, Devonshire-place, in the parish of St. Mary-le-bone. On the 8th of May, about three o'clock in the afternoon, the prisoner came to the house, and asked if he could see Dr. Young - I said he was not at home - he asked at what time he could see him - I said before half-past nine in the morning, or after five in the afternoon - he then asked if he could leave a note for him - I said Yes, and showed him into the dining-room, and went in search of paper, which I gave him, and left the room - he came out of the dining-room, and then went away - in about half an hour the officer came, and asked if I had missed any plate - I then counted my spoons in the dining-room, and missed three - he asked if I had seen an Irish gentleman - I said I had.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Are you sure of his person? A. Yes - I am a Scotchman; the spoons were on the sideboard.
JAMES GIBBS. I was sent for to take the prisoner, who was in Bond's parlour, and found two books on him - I asked if he had been to any house; he said he had been to Dr. Young's.
PHILIP WEBSTER. I am an officer. Brown gave me this note.
JAMES BROWN. This is the note he delivered me - these are master's spoons; they weigh 11 ozs. and are worth 55s. as old silver, at 5s. an oz. (Note read)
"Sir. - Be so good as to be at home to-morrow about ten o'clock. Your's, R. J. LEE."
GUILTY - DEATH. Aged 32.
[trialAccount t18270531-327]
Third London Jury - before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
1340. CHARLES JONES was indicted for a burglary in the dwelling-house of Mozely Woolf, about eight o'clock at night on the 12th April, and stealing 39l yards of woollen cloth, value 169l.; 41yards of linen cloth, value 2l. 4s., and 41 yards of striped cotton, value 25s, his property.
WILLIAM HENMAN. I am an officer of Cripplegate. On Thursday, the 12th of April, I went there in consequence of information, at a quarter-past eight o'clock in the evening, and saw the prisoner come out of Mr. Woolf's premises, No.11, Aldermanbury; he came out at the front entrance - I was within three yards of him, by the side of the house - I did not see the door open, but heard it shut, and saw him come from it - I am quite sure he came out of the house; he walked across Addle-street, and up Philip-lane; he then ran, and I called Stop thief! at the corner of Philip-lane he turned round, looked at me, and threw down a crow-bar. I have reason to believe he threw it at me, but it did not hit me - I followed, till he got to Moor-lane, in the parish of Cripplegate without, and then I secured him, with the assistance of others - I suppose he had run near a quarter of a mile - I brought him back to the watch-house, in Aldermanbury - I found on him eight skeleton keys, a little jemmy, a knife, and a piece of string; the other officer, who was with me, picked up a crow-bar in the street - I accused him of coming from Mr. Woolf's; he said he had not, I was mistaken; we placed him in the Computer - I and the officer came back and opened Mr. Woolf's door, with one of the skeleton keys which we found on him.
JURY. Q. Did he carry anything with him out of Mr. Woolf's? A. No; when we opened the door there were nine ends of cloth, tied together, ready to be thrown into a cart - on the right-hand of the warehouse is a door which goes into a passage that appeared to have been attempted to be broken open - the wood-work of the partition was broken away. I went on further, and found another door, which was open, and the patent padlock broken off; on the counter there were eight more ends of cloth tied together the same way as the others and with the same sort of string - there was a piece of striped cotton and a piece of sheeting; this is some of the string I found on the prisoner - it is the same as that the cloth was tied with - it does not belong to the prosecutor. I found a great quantity of string on the prisoner; the property removed was of great extent - some was in the passage and some on a shop-board in the warehouse.
Q. Were there a great many persons passing? A. Yes; it was not possible that any other person could have come out, and I mistake the prisoner for him.
Cross-examined by MR. BRODRICK. Q. How long have you been an officer? A. About fifteen months. I found this key opened the door. I saw the other officer open the door. I saw him come out; there were several people in the street - the lamps were alight; he walked out of the house and shut the door - there were no persons between him and me - when he walked away there were three or four persons at one corner and another. I did not lose sight of him, as the street he went down was opposite - there are several turnings between the prosecutor's house and Moor-lane; but I did not lose sight of him - there were two other officers following him. I did not pick up the crow-bar - it was brought to the watch-house. I was not more than two or three yards from him, while he was running. I struck him once or twice, but could not lay hold of him.
JURY. Q. Did he run immediately he left the house. A. No; he walked across the road. I could not take him- he was about three yards from me; I could not reach him. I began to run and so did he.
JAMES TONKIN. I have been an officer for fifteen years. I was with Henman and another officer. We went there by design. I saw the prisoner come out of No.11; he walked across the street, into Philip-lane; Henman stepped pretty quick after him - I followed, and Neal came after me- when we came to the end of Philip-lane I heard a piece of iron fall; a young man took it up; I took it from him, Henman ran down London-wall, up Aldermanbury-postern, across Fore-street, and up Moor-lane; I lost sight of him, but when I got into Moor-lane Henman had got him; we took him to the watch-house, and found these skeleton-keys on him - I am sure he is the same person. I went back to the premises, and found them in the state that has been described; I opened the door myself, with this key; the string round the parcels corresponds with this found on the prisoner.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Are there not thousands of pieces of string like this? A. I suppose there may be; there were no persons by the side of the door, but there were others at the top of the street, and some at the bottom; I suppose there was no person nearer than fifteen or sixteen yards from the door when the prisoner came out - Henman was about three yards from him. I heard him shut the door - he took time to do that; the door was shut I know before he came out; I believe Henman was not certain whether he belonged to the house or not - that is what I understood; we have not particularly talked this business over - we have spoken of it, but I never think much of it after a prisoner is taken up, till he is tried. I lost sight if Henman while I stopped to get the crow-bar; I suppose he was then two or three yards from the prisoner; it is a straight line into Phillip-lane - there is no turning there; I lost sight of the prisoner directly almost; I only saw Henman and him in Philip-lane; he was not more than three or four yards from him.
COURT. Q. Corners, or no corners, do you state, upon your oath, that is the man who came out of the house? A. Yes, I have no doubt of it.
JURY. Q. Was there any other person came out of the house? A. No. A cart came about eight o'clock, and stood by the door-way.
SAMUEL NEAL. I am an officer. I was with the other officers, and saw the prisoner come out of the house - he shut the door after him; what they have stated is correct; these tools were found on the prisoner.
Cross-examined by MR. BRODRICK. Q. What distance were you from the house? A. About five yards; the lamps were alight; there was a lamp just over the house - he crossed the road, into Philip-lane; I was in the middle of the road when he came out; I did not see him taken - I can swear to him, from seeing his face, and seeing him come out of the house; I could not seize him - he was about five yards from me; I did not run at him.
JAMES WEBB. I am porter to Mr. Woolf - he is a warehouseman, in Addle-street. On Thursday, the 12th of April, I left the warehouse at about half-past four o'clock. or a quarter before five, perfectly locked up and secured; I went out at the side door - there was a door leading from the passage to the warehouse - it fastens inside; there is a bar, two bolts, and a double lock in the middle warehouse; there is another door, with a bar and a padlock, which is here. When I went out I fastened the street door with a double lock - I had left all the woollen cloth safe in thewarehouse, as usual, not tied up with string, nor in the passage, but in the cases, and the lined cloth also. I heard of the robbery afterwards, and went to the warehouse; I overtook Mr. Woolf and the officers - when I got there I found the property tied up in bundles.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did you leave any one in the warehouse? A. No. It was Mr. Woolf's dwelling-house.
MOZELY WOOLF. I am a warehouseman - my warehouse is at No. 11, Addle-street, Aldermanbury - I am sure I do not know whether it is in the parish of St. Mary-the-Virgin or no; it is my dwelling-house; my servant sleeps there at night, to guard the property; he sleeps there as my servant; he leaves at five o'clock to go to dinner; he calls at my house in Goodman's-fields, and returns at nine o'clock to sleep there.
MR. PHILLIPS to JAMES WEBB. Q. Did you sleep there that night? A. Yes, with the officer; I slept there the night before; I am always in the habit of sleeping there; I have slept there about eleven weeks altogether; I have slept away, and had slept there about a month before the removal of these goods, but will not swear I slept there constantly; I can swear I did not sleep out of it for four nights in that month; I do not dine or drink tea in the house, but I sleep there to take care of the things.
COURT to MR. WOOLF. Q. At What hour was your attention drawn to this warehouse? A. The officer came, and told me of it about ten o'clock, and I went there; I lound my clothes tied up by threes, and a number of things removed; one piece of sheeting was very much cut with some instrument; there were three hundred or four hundred yards of cloth and kerseymere removed, to the amount of 170l.; my servant slept there by my orders since Mr. Fanchard left.
Cross-examined by MR. BRODRICK. Q. Did he sleep there to take care of your property? A. Yes, that was my object; he was my porter then; he does other things, such as clean boots and shoes, and knives; he slept in the warehouse to take care of the property; I live at No. 26, Mansell-street, Goodman's-fields, not at the warehouse; I do not intend to remove there; I have lived in Mansell-street ten years; it is at Mansell-street he acts as my domestic servant.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. You have sent him there to take care of your goods, and watch the house at night? A. I do not sleep at the warehouse; Goodman's-fields is where I eat my meals; I have taken a lunch, and have dined at Addle-street; if I were asked where I reside, I should say, from such a time to such a time, I should be at Addle-street, and other times at Goodman's-fields; my servant sleeps there, and does other things.
COURT. Q. You have occasionally eat your meals there? A. Yes; and this man is to do any thing there which I bid him; I have no bed there myself.
JURY. Q. Do you pay assessed taxes for it as your dwelling-house? A. Yes.
Prisoner's Defence. I am innocent of it altogether.
GUILTY. Aged 33.
Of stealing, but not in the dwelling-house.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18270531-328]
1341. JOHN LYALL and THOMAS BATTY were indicted for stealing, on the 2d of May, 1 watch, value 3l.; 1 watch-key, value 2d., and 1 watch-ribbon, value 1d., the goods of Thomas Pegg, from his person.
THOMAS PEGG. I was coming from Drury-lane with my wife on the 2d of May - I was in Pickett-street, and a person came to me and asked if my name was Johnson; I said No, and came on to Temple-bar, when another person met me and asked if my name was Johnson; he then took my watch and ran up Shire-lane - my wife had hold of my right arm, and my boy of my left - my wife ran after him, and left my boy with me - I have never seen the watch since - I had had a little liquor.
Cross-examined by MR. BRODRICK. Q. You say you were a little in liquor? A. Yes; I had not seen the two persons together who asked me the question; I had a quarrel that evening, and had been fighting at the Blue Anchor public-house, in Feathers-court, about half an hour before.
Cross-examined by MR. QUIN. Q. You were very drunk, I believe? A. No; I was rather stupid with liquor - I saw my watch go.
ANN ELIZABETH PEGG. I was with my husband - he was stopped by a person in Pickett-street, but I had not hold of his arm at that time, and do not know any thing about it - I had been at the Feathers, and saw Lyall there, leaning against the table - I should not have known Lyall, but he walked all the way close behind us, and before we got to Temple-bar he tapped my husband on the shoulder - I had not hold of his arm, and did not hear what he said; when we got on to Temple-bar Lyall took hold of the flap of his coat, and said, "Is your name Johnson?" I said, "No, you blackguard," and then he took the watch and ran up Shire-lane; I ran after him, and he turned to the left - I am sure he is the man who was in the public-house, and who took the watch.
Cross-examined by MR. QUIN. Q. You say that when he took the watch he said something about Johnson? A. Yes; I never went to a public-house to find out the man. I have said the man had blue on, but I knew him by his face. I saw him again in about an hour, when the officer brought him into the public-house. I did not point out a man of the name of Heath. I never pointed out any man but him.
GEORGE STARR. I was with the prosecutor and his wife, at the public-house. I saw the two prisoners there, they went out with them. I did not walk with them to Temple-bar, but I was in sight of them. I saw the two prisoners walk in company a little way from the prosecutor. I saw Batty accost him first; but was not near enough to hear what he said; I then missed sight of them - I went through the gate-way and went on to Fetter-lane. I afterwards went back to the public-house and found Lyall there.
Cross-examined by MR. QUIN. Q. Was there a person in the public-house named Heath? A. Yes; Mrs. Pegg did not point him out, she only pointed out Lyall.
JAMES LEDGEN. I am a Bow-street patrol. I took Lyall at the corner of Feathers-court. I did not find the watch.
WILLIAM WHEATLEY. I am a Bow-street patrol. I took Batty at the Black-horse, public-house. Seven-dials.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18270531-329]
1342. SAMUEL POWELL was indicted for a rape.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18270531-330]
EIGHTH DAY. FRIDAY, JUNE 8.
Third London Jury - Before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
1343. ELIZABETH JORDAN was indicted for stealing, on the 2d of June, 1 watch, value 2l. 10s.; 1 seal, value 1l. 1s.; 1 watch-key, value 10s.; 1 gold ring, value 10s.; and 1 watch-ribbon, value 1d., the goods of Charles Wales, from his person.
CHARLES WALES. I am a painter, and live with my father, in Gravel-lane. About two o'clock in the morning of the 2d of June, I was returning from the theatre - I had had some refreshment, but I was sober; the prisoner accosted me on Ludgate-hill and asked me to treat her; I asked what she meant, she said, "Give me something to drink," I walked to the corner of Bridge-street, and stood talking with her for two or three minutes. I saw her with my watch in her hand, and she put it into her bosom. I accused her of it - the watchman came up and took her to the watch-house.
WILLIAM SINGLETON. I am a watchman. I saw the prisoner and prosecutor together, talking, at the corner of Bridge-street, the prosecutor had hold of her, and asked for his watch. I took her to the watch-house; the officer found it in her bosom.
JOSEPH STRIBLEHILL. I was officer of the night - The prisoner was brought to the watch-house in custody; the prosecutor appeared sober; I searched the prisoner - this watch was in her bosom.
HENRY BOLTON. I was watch-house-keeper. I saw the prisoner take this watch from her bosom; she had been charged with stealing it, and denied it.
Prisoner's Defence. He went home with me, and afterwards to several public-houses - he gave me his watch as a pledge; I refused to return it, and he called the watchman.
GUILTY. Aged 21.
Transported for Life.
[trialAccount t18270531-331]
1344. WILLIAM PAINTER was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of May, 1 handkerchief, value 1s., the goods of Richard Tooley Chapman, from his person.
RICHARD TOOLEY CHAPMAN. I am a surgeon, and live in Bedford-place, Commercial-road. I was in the Justice-room at the Mansion-house on business, on the 15th of May, about half-past one o'clock, and felt a hand coming round to my watch. I put my hand to protect it, and in a short time I felt a hand at my pocket. I put my hand down and missed my handkerchief. I turned round and saw the prisoner putting his hat on his head, in which I saw a handkerchief. I said "You have my handkerchief;" he said "I have not." I said "I know you have, and I insist upon seeing it, if not, I will call the Marshalman." I put my hand to his hat, and he showed me a silk hand - kerchief, and said "Is this yours." I said No - mine is a cotton one." I called the Marshalman, and the case was heard - this is the handkerchief - it was in his hat.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. On what business had you gone there? A. To make an affidavit; the prisoner was behind me; I felt a hand at my pocket, but I cannot tell what hand it was; there was a crowd of people, some before and some behind me; I turned round, and the prisoner was putting his hat upon his head; no person could have stooped behind me; there was not room - it was not a pressure behind me, it was a fumbling; it might have been done by a person raising his hand up from the ground, but the prisoner was upright when I saw him; he might have stopped and raised a hand from the ground, but I should think he could not.
JURY. Q. Did he deny having it? A. Yes; and he told the Lord Mayor that I said the silk handkerchief was mine - I said I did not; the cotton one was mine - he told the Lord Mayor he picked it up, but he could not, there was not room.
MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. But how do you known he could not have stooped? A. Because the people were so pressed together; he had a silk handkerchief of his own and mine in the bottom of his hat; his handkerchief covered mine; I took mine out of his hat; it has never happened to me to feel any thing under my feet, and to mistake another handkerchief for my own; he, I understand, had been a person in a respectable situation in life, but has left his family.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I found this handkerchief on the floor, and took it up; the prosecutor turned round and took hold of my handkerchief, and said, "This is mine" - I said, "It is not" - he then took hold of the one I had found, and said, "This is mine" - he then gave me in charge.
GUILTY. Aged 32.
Recommended to Mercy by the Jury, having received an excellent character.
Confined Eighteen Months.
[trialAccount t18270531-332]
1345. JOHN MITCHELL was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of May, 1 handkerchief, value 5s., the goods of Richard Hodgson Galloway, from his person.
RICHARD HODGSON GALLOWAY. I am an engineer, and live in West-street, Smithfield. I was in Field-lane, a quarter before seven o'clock in the evening of the 19th of May; I felt something all at once pulling at my pocket - I turned round and saw the prisoner with my handkerchief in his hand, and he shuffled it up his coat - I pushed him against a shop, and accused him of it; he said he knew nothing of it, but he dropped it in two or three minutes, and I collared him, and took him to the watch-house; he had not run; I saw the handkerchief in his hand, and saw him drop it.
Prisoner. Q. Was there not another gentleman with you? A. Yes, my brother - and there was a boy with the prisoner, and I saw the handkerchief shifting from that boy to the prisoner: my belief is, that the other picked my pocket and passed it to the prisoner - I seized the prisoner, and put him up against a shutter, that in case he dropped it, I might see it - I believe I did state at Guildhall, that the boy handed the handkerchief to him; the other was nearer to me; I know the boy's face; I have seen him there.
THOMAS WHITE. I am an officer. I received charge of the prisoner.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I was standing in the lane with my hands in my pockets, looking at some men mending the sewer. I saw the prosecutor and a gentleman pass me; the handkerchief was thrown, and rested on my elbow. I never moved, nor pulled my hands out of my pockets; there were a number of people who said I was not the man.
GUILTY. Aged 28.
Recommended to mercy by the Prosecutor.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18270531-333]
1346. JESSE JAMES BERESFORD was indicted for a misdemeanor.
NOT GUILTY.
